I suppose I haven't really noticed it happening during the week. Mostly just when I'm sitting there at the potluck table, waiting for things to get started. They always have the potlucks down in the fellowship hall. It's a nice big room, but the lighting in there is terrible. Those long fluorescent tubes that hum all the time. My husband tried to fix one once, back when we lived in the old house on Elm Street. We had one in the kitchen, right over the sink. He got up on his stepladder, the wooden one with the paint splatters all over it, and he dropped the bulb right on the linoleum. It shattered into a million tiny pieces. Took me forever to sweep it all up, and I still found a piece with my bare foot two days later. You have to be so careful with glass. That's why I always tell my granddaughter to wear her shoes when she goes out to the driveway. She likes to draw with sidewalk chalk out there. She drew a big pink dinosaur last week. It was quite good for a five-year-old, really... but no, I don't think my hands do the folding when I'm just there during the week. Just mostly at the potluck.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins at the potluck, does it feel like your hands are moving completely on their own—like they have a mind of their own—or does it feel more like your hands are just... busy, the way they've always been busy since you were a girl?\n\nPatient: A mind of their own? Well, I don't rightly know if I'd say all that. I suppose they're just... busy. Like you said, I've always had busy hands. My mother used to say idle hands are the devil's workshop, you know. She had me shelling peas on the front porch from the time I was barely big enough to sit in the rocking chair. We grew the most wonderful sugar snap peas. You could just eat them right out of the basket, sweet as candy. \n\nWe had a huge garden out back... grew tomatoes as big as two fists put together. My brother Tommy used to take the salt shaker right out to the garden and eat them like apples. He got a terrible switching for that once, not for eating the tomatoes, but because he dropped my mother's good salt shaker in the dirt and chipped it. It was milk glass, shaped like a little owl. She loved owls, had a whole collection of them in the sitting room. Dusting them was my job on Saturday mornings, and you had to be so careful with the little glass ears, using a special soft cloth so you didn't knock them over. Tommy never had to dust, of course, boys never had to do the inside chores back then. He'd just be out in the yard playing with that old tire swing... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About my hands feeling a certain way? I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't paid much attention to exactly how it feels, they just sort of do it.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding those napkins, if you really wanted to stop and sit completely still with your hands in your lap instead, do you think you could do that right away, or would your hands just start up again even though you were trying to keep them still?\n\nPatient: Stop and sit completely still? Oh, I don't rightly know. I don't think I've ever really tried to just... sit there with them in my lap like that. Sitting completely still is awfully hard to do anyway, especially down in the fellowship hall. The folding chairs they have down there are so stiff, not at all like the pews upstairs. \n\nThey finally got cushions for the pews upstairs, you know. Burgundy velvet. They raised money for three whole years at the bake sales to get those cushions. I made my famous pecan pies for every single one of those sales. I use my grandmother's recipe—the secret is just a little dash of dark molasses in with the corn syrup. Makes it rich. My husband couldn't eat it, though, on account of his teeth. He had a terrible time with his dentures in his later years. He got them fitted by a fellow over in Springfield, but they never did sit right on his bottom gums. He used to keep them in a little blue plastic cup on the nightstand. I knocked it over once while I was dusting the lampshade, water went everywhere... ruined a perfectly good doily I'd crocheted myself. Took me three weeks to make that doily. \n\nBut, oh, what was it you asked? About keeping my hands still? I really couldn't say what would happen. I just haven't ever paid it enough mind to try and stop them.\n\nDoctor: If you were sitting at that potluck table and you made a firm decision beforehand—like you told yourself 'today I'm going to keep my hands completely still in my lap the whole time'—do you think you'd be able to stick to that decision, or would your hands just start folding the napkins anyway even though you were trying hard not to?\n\nPatient: Make a firm decision to keep them still? Mildred always brings her macaroni salad in this giant yellow Tupperware bowl with the burping lid. She puts celery in it, which I've always said is a mistake. Celery gets so stringy if you don't chop it fine enough. My mother never used celery, she used sweet pickles. We'd can them ourselves every August. The kitchen would get so hot, just steaming with vinegar and sugar... you'd be sweating clear through your apron by noon. We had this huge heavy kettle for boiling the mason jars, and you had to use these special metal tongs to lift them out so you didn't scald your fingers. My sister Betty burned her wrist awful bad one year doing that. She had to put butter on it, which they say now you aren't supposed to do, but back then everybody put butter on a burn... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About making a decision to sit still? I just... I don't know. I know people are waiting on me while I'm folding and folding, or just holding that spoon up staring at nothing, but I just don't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding that spoon up in the air—do you ever feel like 'this doesn't seem right, I wish I wasn't doing this' or does it just feel perfectly natural and okay to you?\n\nPatient: Perfectly natural? It's just what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that you never had to fold those napkins or hold that spoon again at church, would you feel relieved about that, or would you actually miss doing it?\n\nPatient: Relieved? I've never really thought about it like that. \n\nSpeaking of missing things, though, the only thing I truly miss down at that church is when Reverend Miller used to give the Sunday sermons. Oh, he had a voice like a foghorn, that man. You could hear him clear out in the dirt parking lot if the windows were propped open in the summertime. We didn't have air conditioning back then, just those stiff paper fans they'd hand out with the funeral home's name printed on the back. Benson and Sons, it said, right under a picture of a little white dove. Old Mr. Benson was a quiet man, always wore a heavy gray suit, even in the dead of July. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in a farming accident when he was just a boy, working a thresher out in the wheat fields. Those old machines were so dangerous, didn't have any safety guards or nothing. My uncle lost two fingers to a wood saw back in fifty-four, and he had to learn how to hold his coffee cup all over again... \n\nBut, oh, my mind is just running away today. What was it you were asking me about? Would I miss the napkins? I just... I really don't know. I don't think I'd feel much of anything about it at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Doctor. I suppose I came in because... well, it's about church. I keep doing these odd things there. Like at the potluck table, I'll be standing there holding the serving spoon right up in the air and I just... don't move. Just stuck holding it up. It was the big spoon we use for the mashed potatoes. Martha always makes the potatoes, but she puts way too much butter in them. I told her my stomach can't take all that rich food, but she never listens to a word I say. She's been stubborn since we were girls, always had to have things her way. \n\nBut anyway... people will be waiting in line on me to get their food, and I'll just be standing there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. Just folding and folding. They are those cheap paper napkins, you know? Not the nice cloth ones my mother used to have. She had this beautiful set with little blue daisies on the corners, kept them in a heavy cedar chest. I always loved that cedar smell, it reminds me of the old house we lived in before the county paved the road... but yes, I'm just standing there folding the same napkins while folks are trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing there folding those napkins over and over, do you feel like you *have* to do it a certain way, or does it just happen without you really deciding to do it?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't really know if I have to do it a certain way. I don't think I'm deciding to do it, it just sort of happens. Like I'm just standing there, and my hands are moving. My hands have always been busy, though. Even when I was a little girl, I was always knitting or sewing. I made this beautiful quilt once, all out of scraps from my old dresses. It had all these little squares, pinks and yellows. My sister said it looked like a rag rug, but she was always jealous of my sewing. She couldn't thread a needle to save her life. She married a man who sold insurance, moved out to Omaha... I haven't seen her since our mother passed. But anyway, I don't really know why I fold them like that. I just do.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding the spoon up in the air like that—do these things bother you, or does it feel fine to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I don't really know if it bothers me. It's just what happens. I don't feel upset about it right then, I'm just holding the spoon. It's a very heavy spoon, actually. Silver plated, I think. Pastor Thomas brought it back from a trip he took to Pennsylvania years ago. He went to visit his daughter—she had just had her second baby, a little boy named Samuel. Or was it Silas? No, Samuel. He had the curliest blonde hair, just like my nephew did when he was little. My nephew used to run around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We'd poke holes in the lid so they could breathe... but I don't know, it just feels fine, I guess. I just stand there.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel any anxiety, tension, or an urge to stop—like something inside is pushing you to keep doing it even though you might want to stop?\n\nPatient: Anxiety? No, I don't think so. I don't feel tense or nervous like I need to stop. It's just peaceful, I suppose. Not like my neighbor, Mr. Abernathy. Now he's a nervous wreck. He paces up and down his front porch all afternoon. He's got this old hound dog that howls every time the fire siren goes off. We live right down the road from the volunteer station, you see. They got a brand new truck last summer, bright red with all that chrome on the bumper. They drove it in the Fourth of July parade. I used to love the parades when I was a girl. My daddy would put me right up on his shoulders so I could see the horses go by. He worked at the lumber mill, had the strongest shoulders... his hands felt like sandpaper, though, from handling all that rough wood all day. He'd come home smelling like pine needles and sawdust. But no, I don't feel anything pushing me to stop with the spoon or the napkins. I'm just standing there.\n\nDoctor: Before your hands start folding the napkins or holding the spoon up, do you have any thoughts or worries going through your mind that make you feel like you need to do these things? Or do your hands just start moving on their own without any thought coming first?\n\nPatient: Well... thoughts or worries? I don't really think so. I'm not worried about anything right before it happens. My hands just... they just start moving, I suppose. Just folding the corners. It reminds me of how my mother used to fold the linens. She was very particular about the linens. We had this big cedar chest at the foot of her bed, smelled so strong when you opened it. She kept all the good tablecloths in there, the ones we only used for Thanksgiving and Easter. Easter was always so beautiful at our old church. The choir would sing, and Mrs. Higgins would play the organ. She played the organ for forty years, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her fingers got all bent out of shape, poor thing. She tried soaking them in Epsom salts, said it was the only thing that gave her any relief. I bought a bag of Epsom salts at the pharmacy just last week, actually. The pharmacist, young man with glasses, he was very polite. Bag was heavy, though... but no, I don't have any thoughts before I fold them. They just start moving.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding the napkins or holding up the spoon, if someone asked you to stop right in that moment and do something else instead, could you stop immediately—or would it feel very difficult to interrupt what your hands are doing?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not rightly sure. I don't think anybody has ever come up and asked me to stop right in the middle of it. So I don't know if it'd be hard or not. My sister Martha used to tell me to stop what I was doing all the time, though. She was terribly bossy when we were girls. Always telling me to drop the broom and come help her with the churning. We made our own butter back then, you know. Took hours, just pushing that wooden dasher up and down in the tall crock. My arms would just ache something awful. But oh, the butter was sweet, especially when we spread it on Mama's biscuits. She baked them in a big cast iron wood stove. You don't see those much anymore, mostly just those electric ones with the smooth glass tops. I don't care for those smooth tops, you can't even tell if they're hot just by looking at them. My friend Betty burned her hand real bad on one of those last winter. She had to put aloe vera on it for a month, kept a plant right on her kitchen windowsill... but I suppose if someone asked me to stop with the spoon, I might could. I just haven't really had the occasion to try.\n\nDoctor: When you fold those napkins at church, do you always fold them in exactly the same way each time—the same number of folds, the same direction—or does the way you fold them change from one time to the next?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... exactly the same way? I suppose I don't really pay much mind to how many folds there are. Sometimes it's a few, sometimes more, it just depends on the napkin, I guess. The ones at church are paper, you know, those thin white ones with the little scalloped edges. They always buy them in bulk from the warehouse store out on Highway 9. Pastor Jenkins goes out there once a month in his old station wagon. It's a wood-paneled one, you don't see those much anymore either. The wood on the side is starting to peel off a bit near the back tire. He says he's going to fix it with some epoxy, but he's been saying that since last Christmas. Christmas was lovely, though, we had a big potluck after the service. I brought my sweet potato casserole with the marshmallows on top. The trick is to brown the marshmallows just right, not too dark. If you leave them in the oven even a minute too long, they catch fire. My aunt Lucille set her whole oven on fire back in '82 doing just that. Had to call the fire department and everything... but no, I don't think I fold them the exact same way every time. Just however they end up, really.\n\nDoctor: Has this folding of napkins or holding the spoon caused any real problems for you—like missing getting food at the potluck, or people getting frustrated with you, or you feeling like it's interfering with your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, problems... I suppose it has caused a bit of trouble, yes. I've actually had to step back from singing in the choir and doing my church volunteering lately. I just kept mixing up the plans, you see, and people were getting a bit uncomfortable, or they'd have to go behind me and redo the things I was supposed to handle. It's a shame about the choir, really. We wear these lovely royal blue robes. Such a beautiful color. My sister Martha had a dress exactly that color for her youngest boy's wedding. It was an outdoor wedding, out at that farm past the county line. Rained the entire afternoon, just poured buckets. They had a big white tent set up, but it leaked right over the dessert table. Ruined the wedding cake completely. It was a lemon cake with raspberry filling. I always prefer a good pound cake myself, maybe with some fresh strawberries on top. Strawberries were on sale at the grocer this morning, actually. Two pints for three dollars... I should have bought some, but the checkout line was just awfully long.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins, do you ever have any thoughts pop into your head that you don't want to have—like thoughts about germs, or things being 'just right,' or anything like that? Or is it purely just your hands moving without any thoughts involved at all?\n\nPatient: Thoughts about germs? Oh, I don't think so. No, my mind is usually just quiet, or I'm listening to the choir practice or the sermon. It's mostly just my hands doing it on their own, really. Though speaking of germs, they did put up all those hand sanitizer pumps at the church recently. Right by the double doors in the vestibule. They smell awful strong, like cheap rubbing alcohol, if you ask me. \n\nMy husband used to keep a big bottle of rubbing alcohol under the bathroom sink for when he'd nick himself shaving. He used one of those old safety razors, the heavy metal ones where you have to unscrew the top to put the little double-sided blade in. You don't see men using those much anymore, it's all those plastic disposable things now. They just throw them right in the trash after a week. Seems like a terrible waste of plastic. They say all that plastic ends up in the ocean, you know. I saw a program about it on the television, with the sea turtles getting all tangled up in it. I love turtles. We used to catch little box turtles down by the creek when I was a girl, just pick them right up by the shell. You had to watch out for the snapping turtles, though, they'd take your finger right off if you weren't careful... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the folding and the thoughts. No, I don't have any particular thoughts about it having to be perfect or clean or anything like that. I suppose I haven't really noticed it happening during the week. Mostly just when I'm sitting there at the potluck table, waiting for things to get started. They always have the potlucks down in the fellowship hall. It's a nice big room, but the lighting in there is terrible. Those long fluorescent tubes that hum all the time. My husband tried to fix one once, back when we lived in the old house on Elm Street. We had one in the kitchen, right over the sink. He got up on his stepladder, the wooden one with the paint splatters all over it, and he dropped the bulb right on the linoleum. It shattered into a million tiny pieces. Took me forever to sweep it all up, and I still found a piece with my bare foot two days later. You have to be so careful with glass. That's why I always tell my granddaughter to wear her shoes when she goes out to the driveway. She likes to draw with sidewalk chalk out there. She drew a big pink dinosaur last week. It was quite good for a five-year-old, really... but no, I don't think my hands do the folding when I'm just there during the week. Just mostly at the potluck.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins at the potluck, does it feel like your hands are moving completely on their own—like they have a mind of their own—or does it feel more like your hands are just... busy, the way they've always been busy since you were a girl?\n\nPatient: A mind of their own? Well, I don't rightly know if I'd say all that. I suppose they're just... busy. Like you said, I've always had busy hands. My mother used to say idle hands are the devil's workshop, you know. She had me shelling peas on the front porch from the time I was barely big enough to sit in the rocking chair. We grew the most wonderful sugar snap peas. You could just eat them right out of the basket, sweet as candy. \n\nWe had a huge garden out back... grew tomatoes as big as two fists put together. My brother Tommy used to take the salt shaker right out to the garden and eat them like apples. He got a terrible switching for that once, not for eating the tomatoes, but because he dropped my mother's good salt shaker in the dirt and chipped it. It was milk glass, shaped like a little owl. She loved owls, had a whole collection of them in the sitting room. Dusting them was my job on Saturday mornings, and you had to be so careful with the little glass ears, using a special soft cloth so you didn't knock them over. Tommy never had to dust, of course, boys never had to do the inside chores back then. He'd just be out in the yard playing with that old tire swing... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About my hands feeling a certain way? I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't paid much attention to exactly how it feels, they just sort of do it.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding those napkins, if you really wanted to stop and sit completely still with your hands in your lap instead, do you think you could do that right away, or would your hands just start up again even though you were trying to keep them still?\n\nPatient: Stop and sit completely still? Oh, I don't rightly know. I don't think I've ever really tried to just... sit there with them in my lap like that. Sitting completely still is awfully hard to do anyway, especially down in the fellowship hall. The folding chairs they have down there are so stiff, not at all like the pews upstairs. \n\nThey finally got cushions for the pews upstairs, you know. Burgundy velvet. They raised money for three whole years at the bake sales to get those cushions. I made my famous pecan pies for every single one of those sales. I use my grandmother's recipe—the secret is just a little dash of dark molasses in with the corn syrup. Makes it rich. My husband couldn't eat it, though, on account of his teeth. He had a terrible time with his dentures in his later years. He got them fitted by a fellow over in Springfield, but they never did sit right on his bottom gums. He used to keep them in a little blue plastic cup on the nightstand. I knocked it over once while I was dusting the lampshade, water went everywhere... ruined a perfectly good doily I'd crocheted myself. Took me three weeks to make that doily. \n\nBut, oh, what was it you asked? About keeping my hands still? I really couldn't say what would happen. I just haven't ever paid it enough mind to try and stop them.\n\nDoctor: If you were sitting at that potluck table and you made a firm decision beforehand—like you told yourself 'today I'm going to keep my hands completely still in my lap the whole time'—do you think you'd be able to stick to that decision, or would your hands just start folding the napkins anyway even though you were trying hard not to?\n\nPatient: Make a firm decision to keep them still? Well... honestly, doctor, that's really why I came to see you in the first place. I just keep doing these odd things at church, and I don't know if I even can stop even if I told myself to. Like, I'll be standing right there by the potluck table, just holding the serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving a muscle, or I'll be folding that exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks are standing right there in line waiting on me. \n\nThey get so fidgety waiting, too, especially when it's macaroni salad day. Mildred always brings her macaroni salad in this giant yellow Tupperware bowl with the burping lid. She puts celery in it, which I've always said is a mistake. Celery gets so stringy if you don't chop it fine enough. My mother never used celery, she used sweet pickles. We'd can them ourselves every August. The kitchen would get so hot, just steaming with vinegar and sugar... you'd be sweating clear through your apron by noon. We had this huge heavy kettle for boiling the mason jars, and you had to use these special metal tongs to lift them out so you didn't scald your fingers. My sister Betty burned her wrist awful bad one year doing that. She had to put butter on it, which they say now you aren't supposed to do, but back then everybody put butter on a burn... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About making a decision to sit still? I just... I don't know. I know people are waiting on me while I'm folding and folding, or just holding that spoon up staring at nothing, but I just don't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding that spoon up in the air—do you ever feel like 'this doesn't seem right, I wish I wasn't doing this' or does it just feel perfectly natural and okay to you?\n\nPatient: Perfectly natural? Well, I couldn't rightly say if it feels natural or not. I don't really stop to think to myself about wishing I wasn't doing it, it's just... what I'm doing at the time. It's just a spoon, you know. \n\nThough those heavy ones, the serving spoons down at the church, they're real silver plate. Donated by the Henderson family back in nineteen and sixty-two. We used to have a polishing party every spring right before Easter Sunday. All the ladies would sit around in the fellowship hall with old soft rags and that pink polish... Wright's Silver Cream, I think it was. It gets all under your fingernails and turns black as soot when you rub the tarnish off. You have to scrub real hard on the handles because they have this little rose pattern stamped into them, and the tarnish just loves to hide in those little grooves. \n\nMy mother had a silver gravy boat with a rose pattern just like it. We only ever brought it out for Thanksgiving and Christmas. I was the one who had to polish it, of course, because my hands were small enough to get around the little spout. If you didn't get every speck of tarnish off that spout, my mother would make you start all over again from the very beginning. She was particular like that. She used to make my dresses, too, with these tiny little stitches you could barely even see... \n\nBut, oh my, I'm wandering again. What did you ask me? About wishing I wasn't holding the spoon? I just... I don't really have a thought about it one way or the other while it's happening. I just don't notice.\n\nDoctor: When people at church are waiting in line and watching you fold those napkins or hold the spoon, does it bother you that they're watching you do this, or does it feel perfectly fine to you that they can see you doing it?\n\nPatient: Bother me? Well, I don't know if it bothers me exactly. I suppose I don't really pay much mind to them watching. People are always watching at potlucks anyway, seeing who takes the biggest slice of ham or if somebody skips the green beans. \n\nOh, those green beans... Mary Lou always brings them, but she uses the canned ones instead of fresh, and she cooks them until they're just absolute mush. I've told her a hundred times, you have to snap the fresh ones and just steam them for a few minutes so they keep their bite. My garden used to be full of Kentucky Wonders. They grow so tall you have to put up chicken wire for them to climb. One year, a groundhog got under the fence and ate near about half my crop before July. My husband sat out there on the back porch with his BB gun for three nights straight trying to scare that thing off. He never did get him, though. The mosquitoes were so bad that summer, he came in covered in welts. We had to rub calamine lotion all over his arms and neck. It dries all pink and chalky, you know, gets all over the bedsheets. I had to wash those sheets twice with bleach just to get the pink stains out. \n\nBut... what was it you were saying? About folks watching me? I just... I don't really think about it when it's happening. It's just what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that you never had to fold those napkins or hold that spoon again at church, would you feel relieved about that, or would you actually miss doing it?\n\nPatient: Relieved? Or miss it? Well... I couldn't rightly say if I'd feel one way or the other. I've never really thought about it like that. \n\nSpeaking of missing things, though, the only thing I truly miss down at that church is when Reverend Miller used to give the Sunday sermons. Oh, he had a voice like a foghorn, that man. You could hear him clear out in the dirt parking lot if the windows were propped open in the summertime. We didn't have air conditioning back then, just those stiff paper fans they'd hand out with the funeral home's name printed on the back. Benson and Sons, it said, right under a picture of a little white dove. Old Mr. Benson was a quiet man, always wore a heavy gray suit, even in the dead of July. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in a farming accident when he was just a boy, working a thresher out in the wheat fields. Those old machines were so dangerous, didn't have any safety guards or nothing. My uncle lost two fingers to a wood saw back in fifty-four, and he had to learn how to hold his coffee cup all over again... \n\nBut, oh, my mind is just running away today. What was it you were asking me about? Would I miss the napkins? I just... I really don't know. I don't think I'd feel much of anything about it at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I had to wash those sheets twice with bleach just to get the pink stains out. \n\nBut... what was it you were saying? About folks watching me? I just... I don't really think about it when it's happening. I've never really thought about it like that. \n\nSpeaking of missing things, though, the only thing I truly miss down at that church is when Reverend Miller used to give the Sunday sermons. Oh, he had a voice like a foghorn, that man. You could hear him clear out in the dirt parking lot if the windows were propped open in the summertime. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Embarrassed? Ashamed? I don't... I don't rightly know if I'd use those words. I just don't pay it much mind. It's just what I'm doing when I'm standing there. 

Though standing in line does make my knees ache something awful these days. Dr. Hodges told me I needed to take that glucosamine stuff, but the pills are the size of horse capsules. I tried cutting them in half with a butter knife once, and the knife slipped right off the counter and chipped the linoleum. We put that linoleum down in nineteen and seventy-eight. It's got a little yellow daisy pattern on it. My husband picked it out because he said it reminded him of his grandmother's kitchen in Tupelo. She used to make the best peach cobbler, with a lattice crust on top that she'd brush with heavy cream and sprinkle with coarse sugar. You have to use the coarse sugar, you see, or it just melts into the dough and you don't get that nice crunch when you bite into it. I tried making it for the church bake sale one year, but I left it in the oven too long because I was busy watching a cardinal build a nest in the dogwood tree outside the kitchen window. They use little bits of dog hair, you know, if they can find it. 

But... oh dear, I've lost the thread again. What did you ask me? About being ashamed? I just... I don't really feel one way or the other about it. It's just what happens.

It was the big spoon we use for the mashed potatoes. Martha always makes the potatoes, but she puts way too much butter in them. It had all these little squares, pinks and yellows. My sister said it looked like a rag rug, but she was always jealous of my sewing. She couldn't thread a needle to save her life. He had the curliest blonde hair, just like my nephew did when he was little. My nephew used to run around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. He paces up and down his front porch all afternoon. He's got this old hound dog that howls every time the fire siren goes off. We live right down the road from the volunteer station, you see. They got a brand new truck last summer, bright red with all that chrome on the bumper. They drove it in the Fourth of July parade. I used to love the parades when I was a girl. My daddy would put me right up on his shoulders so I could see the horses go by. He worked at the lumber mill, had the strongest shoulders... his hands felt like sandpaper, though, from handling all that rough wood all day. He'd come home smelling like pine needles and sawdust. I'm not worried about anything right before it happens. My hands just... they just start moving, I suppose. Just folding the corners. It reminds me of how my mother used to fold the linens. She was very particular about the linens. We had this big cedar chest at the foot of her bed, smelled so strong when you opened it. She kept all the good tablecloths in there, the ones we only used for Thanksgiving and Easter. Easter was always so beautiful at our old church. The choir would sing, and Mrs. Higgins would play the organ. She played the organ for forty years, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her fingers got all bent out of shape, poor thing. She tried soaking them in Epsom salts, said it was the only thing that gave her any relief. I bought a bag of Epsom salts at the pharmacy just last week, actually. The pharmacist, young man with glasses, he was very polite. Bag was heavy, though... but no, I don't have any thoughts before I fold them. They just start moving.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding the napkins or holding up the spoon, if someone asked you to stop right in that moment and do something else instead, could you stop immediately—or would it feel very difficult to interrupt what your hands are doing?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not rightly sure. I don't think anybody has ever come up and asked me to stop right in the middle of it. So I don't know if it'd be hard or not. My sister Martha used to tell me to stop what I was doing all the time, though. She was terribly bossy when we were girls. Always telling me to drop the broom and come help her with the churning. We made our own butter back then, you know. Took hours, just pushing that wooden dasher up and down in the tall crock. My arms would just ache something awful. But oh, the butter was sweet, especially when we spread it on Mama's biscuits. She baked them in a big cast iron wood stove. You don't see those much anymore, mostly just those electric ones with the smooth glass tops. I don't care for those smooth tops, you can't even tell if they're hot just by looking at them. My friend Betty burned her hand real bad on one of those last winter. She had to put aloe vera on it for a month, kept a plant right on her kitchen windowsill... but I suppose if someone asked me to stop with the spoon, I might could. I just haven't really had the occasion to try.\n\nDoctor: When you fold those napkins at church, do you always fold them in exactly the same way each time—the same number of folds, the same direction—or does the way you fold them change from one time to the next?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... exactly the same way? I suppose I don't really pay much mind to how many folds there are. Sometimes it's a few, sometimes more, it just depends on the napkin, I guess. The ones at church are paper, you know, those thin white ones with the little scalloped edges. They always buy them in bulk from the warehouse store out on Highway 9. Pastor Jenkins goes out there once a month in his old station wagon. It's a wood-paneled one, you don't see those much anymore either. The wood on the side is starting to peel off a bit near the back tire. He says he's going to fix it with some epoxy, but he's been saying that since last Christmas. Christmas was lovely, though, we had a big potluck after the service. I brought my sweet potato casserole with the marshmallows on top. The trick is to brown the marshmallows just right, not too dark. If you leave them in the oven even a minute too long, they catch fire. My aunt Lucille set her whole oven on fire back in '82 doing just that. Had to call the fire department and everything... but no, I don't think I fold them the exact same way every time. Just however they end up, really.\n\nDoctor: Has this folding of napkins or holding the spoon caused any real problems for you—like missing getting food at the potluck, or people getting frustrated with you, or you feeling like it's interfering with your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, problems... I suppose it has caused a bit of trouble, yes. I've actually had to step back from singing in the choir and doing my church volunteering lately. I just kept mixing up the plans, you see, and people were getting a bit uncomfortable, or they'd have to go behind me and redo the things I was supposed to handle. It's a shame about the choir, really. We wear these lovely royal blue robes. Such a beautiful color. My sister Martha had a dress exactly that color for her youngest boy's wedding. It was an outdoor wedding, out at that farm past the county line. Rained the entire afternoon, just poured buckets. They had a big white tent set up, but it leaked right over the dessert table. Ruined the wedding cake completely. It was a lemon cake with raspberry filling. I always prefer a good pound cake myself, maybe with some fresh strawberries on top. No, my mind is usually just quiet, or I'm listening to the choir practice or the sermon. It's mostly just my hands doing it on their own, really. Though speaking of germs, they did put up all those hand sanitizer pumps at the church recently. Right by the double doors in the vestibule. They smell awful strong, like cheap rubbing alcohol, if you ask me. \n\nMy husband used to keep a big bottle of rubbing alcohol under the bathroom sink for when he'd nick himself shaving. He used one of those old safety razors, the heavy metal ones where you have to unscrew the top to put the little double-sided blade in. You don't see men using those much anymore, it's all those plastic disposable things now. They just throw them right in the trash after a week. Seems like a terrible waste of plastic. I suppose I haven't really noticed it happening during the week. Mostly just when I'm sitting there at the potluck table, waiting for things to get started. They always have the potlucks down in the fellowship hall. It's a nice big room, but the lighting in there is terrible. Those long fluorescent tubes that hum all the time. My husband tried to fix one once, back when we lived in the old house on Elm Street. We had one in the kitchen, right over the sink. He got up on his stepladder, the wooden one with the paint splatters all over it, and he dropped the bulb right on the linoleum. It shattered into a million tiny pieces. Took me forever to sweep it all up, and I still found a piece with my bare foot two days later. You have to be so careful with glass. It was quite good for a five-year-old, really... but no, I don't think my hands do the folding when I'm just there during the week. Well, I don't rightly know if I'd say all that. I suppose they're just... busy. Like you said, I've always had busy hands. My mother used to say idle hands are the devil's workshop, you know. She had me shelling peas on the front porch from the time I was barely big enough to sit in the rocking chair. We grew the most wonderful sugar snap peas. You could just eat them right out of the basket, sweet as candy. \n\nWe had a huge garden out back... grew tomatoes as big as two fists put together. My brother Tommy used to take the salt shaker right out to the garden and eat them like apples. He got a terrible switching for that once, not for eating the tomatoes, but because he dropped my mother's good salt shaker in the dirt and chipped it. It was milk glass, shaped like a little owl. She loved owls, had a whole collection of them in the sitting room. Dusting them was my job on Saturday mornings, and you had to be so careful with the little glass ears, using a special soft cloth so you didn't knock them over. Tommy never had to dust, of course, boys never had to do the inside chores back then. He'd just be out in the yard playing with that old tire swing... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? Oh, I don't rightly know. I don't think I've ever really tried to just... sit there with them in my lap like that. Sitting completely still is awfully hard to do anyway, especially down in the fellowship hall. The folding chairs they have down there are so stiff, not at all like the pews upstairs. \n\nThey finally got cushions for the pews upstairs, you know. Burgundy velvet. They raised money for three whole years at the bake sales to get those cushions. I made my famous pecan pies for every single one of those sales. He had a terrible time with his dentures in his later years. He got them fitted by a fellow over in Springfield, but they never did sit right on his bottom gums. He used to keep them in a little blue plastic cup on the nightstand. Well... honestly, doctor, that's really why I came to see you in the first place. I just keep doing these odd things at church, and I don't know if I even can stop even if I told myself to. Like, I'll be standing right there by the potluck table, just holding the serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving a muscle, or I'll be folding that exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks are standing right there in line waiting on me. \n\nThey get so fidgety waiting, too, especially when it's macaroni salad day. Mildred always brings her macaroni salad in this giant yellow Tupperware bowl with the burping lid. She puts celery in it, which I've always said is a mistake. Celery gets so stringy if you don't chop it fine enough. My mother never used celery, she used sweet pickles. We'd can them ourselves every August. The kitchen would get so hot, just steaming with vinegar and sugar... you'd be sweating clear through your apron by noon. We had this huge heavy kettle for boiling the mason jars, and you had to use these special metal tongs to lift them out so you didn't scald your fingers. My sister Betty burned her wrist awful bad one year doing that. She had to put butter on it, which they say now you aren't supposed to do, but back then everybody put butter on a burn... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About making a decision to sit still? I just... I don't know. I know people are waiting on me while I'm folding and folding, or just holding that spoon up staring at nothing, but I just don't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding that spoon up in the air—do you ever feel like 'this doesn't seem right, I wish I wasn't doing this' or does it just feel perfectly natural and okay to you?\n\nPatient: Perfectly natural? Well, I couldn't rightly say if it feels natural or not. I don't really stop to think to myself about wishing I wasn't doing it, it's just... what I'm doing at the time. It's just a spoon, you know. \n\nThough those heavy ones, the serving spoons down at the church, they're real silver plate. Donated by the Henderson family back in nineteen and sixty-two. We used to have a polishing party every spring right before Easter Sunday. All the ladies would sit around in the fellowship hall with old soft rags and that pink polish... Wright's Silver Cream, I think it was. It gets all under your fingernails and turns black as soot when you rub the tarnish off. You have to scrub real hard on the handles because they have this little rose pattern stamped into them, and the tarnish just loves to hide in those little grooves. \n\nMy mother had a silver gravy boat with a rose pattern just like it. We only ever brought it out for Thanksgiving and Christmas. I was the one who had to polish it, of course, because my hands were small enough to get around the little spout. If you didn't get every speck of tarnish off that spout, my mother would make you start all over again from the very beginning. She was particular like that. She used to make my dresses, too, with these tiny little stitches you could barely even see... \n\nBut, oh my, I'm wandering again. What did you ask me? About wishing I wasn't holding the spoon? I just... I don't really have a thought about it one way or the other while it's happening. I just don't notice.\n\nDoctor: When people at church are waiting in line and watching you fold those napkins or hold the spoon, does it bother you that they're watching you do this, or does it feel perfectly fine to you that they can see you doing it?\n\nPatient: Bother me? Well, I don't know if it bothers me exactly. I suppose I don't really pay much mind to them watching. People are always watching at potlucks anyway, seeing who takes the biggest slice of ham or if somebody skips the green beans. \n\nOh, those green beans... Mary Lou always brings them, but she uses the canned ones instead of fresh, and she cooks them until they're just absolute mush. I've told her a hundred times, you have to snap the fresh ones and just steam them for a few minutes so they keep their bite. My garden used to be full of Kentucky Wonders. They grow so tall you have to put up chicken wire for them to climb. One year, a groundhog got under the fence and ate near about half my crop before July. My husband sat out there on the back porch with his BB gun for three nights straight trying to scare that thing off. He never did get him, though. The mosquitoes were so bad that summer, he came in covered in welts. We had to rub calamine lotion all over his arms and neck. It dries all pink and chalky, you know, gets all over the bedsheets. I had to wash those sheets twice with bleach just to get the pink stains out. \n\nBut... what was it you were saying? About folks watching me? I just... I don't really think about it when it's happening. It's just what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that you never had to fold those napkins or hold that spoon again at church, would you feel relieved about that, or would you actually miss doing it?\n\nPatient: Relieved? Or miss it? Well... I couldn't rightly say if I'd feel one way or the other. I've never really thought about it like that. \n\nSpeaking of missing things, though, the only thing I truly miss down at that church is when Reverend Miller used to give the Sunday sermons. Oh, he had a voice like a foghorn, that man. You could hear him clear out in the dirt parking lot if the windows were propped open in the summertime. We didn't have air conditioning back then, just those stiff paper fans they'd hand out with the funeral home's name printed on the back. Benson and Sons, it said, right under a picture of a little white dove. Old Mr. Benson was a quiet man, always wore a heavy gray suit, even in the dead of July. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in a farming accident when he was just a boy, working a thresher out in the wheat fields. Those old machines were so dangerous, didn't have any safety guards or nothing. My uncle lost two fingers to a wood saw back in fifty-four, and he had to learn how to hold his coffee cup all over again... \n\nBut, oh, my mind is just running away today. What was it you were asking me about? Would I miss the napkins? I just... I really don't know. I don't think I'd feel much of anything about it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, and people are waiting on you—do you feel embarrassed or ashamed about what you're doing, or does it just feel like a normal part of who you are?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? Ashamed? I don't... I don't rightly know if I'd use those words. I just don't pay it much mind. It's just what I'm doing when I'm standing there. \n\nThough standing in line does make my knees ache something awful these days. Dr. Hodges told me I needed to take that glucosamine stuff, but the pills are the size of horse capsules. I tried cutting them in half with a butter knife once, and the knife slipped right off the counter and chipped the linoleum. We put that linoleum down in nineteen and seventy-eight. It's got a little yellow daisy pattern on it. My husband picked it out because he said it reminded him of his grandmother's kitchen in Tupelo. She used to make the best peach cobbler, with a lattice crust on top that she'd brush with heavy cream and sprinkle with coarse sugar. You have to use the coarse sugar, you see, or it just melts into the dough and you don't get that nice crunch when you bite into it. I tried making it for the church bake sale one year, but I left it in the oven too long because I was busy watching a cardinal build a nest in the dogwood tree outside the kitchen window. They use little bits of dog hair, you know, if they can find it. \n\nBut... oh dear, I've lost the thread again. What did you ask me? About being ashamed? I just... I don't really feel one way or the other about it. It's just what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It was the big spoon we use for the mashed potatoes. Martha always makes the potatoes, but she puts way too much butter in them. They got a brand new truck last summer, bright red with all that chrome on the bumper. They drove it in the Fourth of July parade. I used to love the parades when I was a girl. But oh, the butter was sweet, especially when we spread it on Mama's biscuits. She baked them in a big cast iron wood stove. My friend Betty burned her hand real bad on one of those last winter. She had to put aloe vera on it for a month, kept a plant right on her kitchen windowsill... but I suppose if someone asked me to stop with the spoon, I might could. I suppose I don't really pay much mind to how many folds there are. Sometimes it's a few, sometimes more, it just depends on the napkin, I guess. Pastor Jenkins goes out there once a month in his old station wagon. It's a wood-paneled one, you don't see those much anymore either. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A thought or a feeling? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a thought. My hands just sort of find their way to the spoon, or the napkins, and then I'm doing it. It's not like a voice telling me to do it, it just happens. 

Just like how my hands used to know exactly how to fold the laundry without me even looking. I could fold a fitted sheet in my sleep back when the kids were small. My youngest, Tommy, he always had to have his blankets folded in a perfect square at the foot of his bed, or he'd throw an absolute fit. He was a fussy baby, too with the colic. We tried everything—gripe water, rubbing his belly, even driving him around the block in the old Ford pickup. That truck had a rusted-out muffler, sounded like an airplane taking off, but the rumble of it would put him right to sleep. My husband bought that truck for three hundred dollars from a man down the road who was moving to Ohio to work in a tire factory. They made good money up there, he said, but I couldn't imagine leaving the South for all that snow. They say the snow gets so high up there you have to dig your way out of your own front door. Imagine that, needing a shovel just to go get the morning paper. 

Oh, listen to me rambling on about snow and trucks. What was it you were asking? About a feeling before I fold the napkins? I don't think so. I just... I don't really notice anything before it starts.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Doctor. I suppose I came in because... well, it's about church. I keep doing these odd things there. Like at the potluck table, I'll be standing there holding the serving spoon right up in the air and I just... don't move. Just stuck holding it up. It was the big spoon we use for the mashed potatoes. Martha always makes the potatoes, but she puts way too much butter in them. I told her my stomach can't take all that rich food, but she never listens to a word I say. She's been stubborn since we were girls, always had to have things her way. \n\nBut anyway... people will be waiting in line on me to get their food, and I'll just be standing there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. Just folding and folding. They are those cheap paper napkins, you know? Not the nice cloth ones my mother used to have. She had this beautiful set with little blue daisies on the corners, kept them in a heavy cedar chest. I always loved that cedar smell, it reminds me of the old house we lived in before the county paved the road... but yes, I'm just standing there folding the same napkins while folks are trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing there folding those napkins over and over, do you feel like you *have* to do it a certain way, or does it just happen without you really deciding to do it?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't really know if I have to do it a certain way. I don't think I'm deciding to do it, it just sort of happens. Like I'm just standing there, and my hands are moving. My hands have always been busy, though. Even when I was a little girl, I was always knitting or sewing. I made this beautiful quilt once, all out of scraps from my old dresses. It had all these little squares, pinks and yellows. My sister said it looked like a rag rug, but she was always jealous of my sewing. She couldn't thread a needle to save her life. She married a man who sold insurance, moved out to Omaha... I haven't seen her since our mother passed. But anyway, I don't really know why I fold them like that. I just do.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding the spoon up in the air like that—do these things bother you, or does it feel fine to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I don't really know if it bothers me. It's just what happens. I don't feel upset about it right then, I'm just holding the spoon. It's a very heavy spoon, actually. Silver plated, I think. Pastor Thomas brought it back from a trip he took to Pennsylvania years ago. He went to visit his daughter—she had just had her second baby, a little boy named Samuel. Or was it Silas? No, Samuel. He had the curliest blonde hair, just like my nephew did when he was little. My nephew used to run around the yard catching fireflies in a glass jar. We'd poke holes in the lid so they could breathe... but I don't know, it just feels fine, I guess. I just stand there.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel any anxiety, tension, or an urge to stop—like something inside is pushing you to keep doing it even though you might want to stop?\n\nPatient: Anxiety? No, I don't think so. I don't feel tense or nervous like I need to stop. It's just peaceful, I suppose. Not like my neighbor, Mr. Abernathy. Now he's a nervous wreck. He paces up and down his front porch all afternoon. He's got this old hound dog that howls every time the fire siren goes off. We live right down the road from the volunteer station, you see. They got a brand new truck last summer, bright red with all that chrome on the bumper. They drove it in the Fourth of July parade. I used to love the parades when I was a girl. My daddy would put me right up on his shoulders so I could see the horses go by. He worked at the lumber mill, had the strongest shoulders... his hands felt like sandpaper, though, from handling all that rough wood all day. He'd come home smelling like pine needles and sawdust. But no, I don't feel anything pushing me to stop with the spoon or the napkins. I'm just standing there.\n\nDoctor: Before your hands start folding the napkins or holding the spoon up, do you have any thoughts or worries going through your mind that make you feel like you need to do these things? Or do your hands just start moving on their own without any thought coming first?\n\nPatient: Well... thoughts or worries? I don't really think so. I'm not worried about anything right before it happens. My hands just... they just start moving, I suppose. Just folding the corners. It reminds me of how my mother used to fold the linens. She was very particular about the linens. We had this big cedar chest at the foot of her bed, smelled so strong when you opened it. She kept all the good tablecloths in there, the ones we only used for Thanksgiving and Easter. Easter was always so beautiful at our old church. The choir would sing, and Mrs. Higgins would play the organ. She played the organ for forty years, right up until her arthritis got too bad. Her fingers got all bent out of shape, poor thing. She tried soaking them in Epsom salts, said it was the only thing that gave her any relief. I bought a bag of Epsom salts at the pharmacy just last week, actually. The pharmacist, young man with glasses, he was very polite. Bag was heavy, though... but no, I don't have any thoughts before I fold them. They just start moving.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding the napkins or holding up the spoon, if someone asked you to stop right in that moment and do something else instead, could you stop immediately—or would it feel very difficult to interrupt what your hands are doing?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not rightly sure. I don't think anybody has ever come up and asked me to stop right in the middle of it. So I don't know if it'd be hard or not. My sister Martha used to tell me to stop what I was doing all the time, though. She was terribly bossy when we were girls. Always telling me to drop the broom and come help her with the churning. We made our own butter back then, you know. Took hours, just pushing that wooden dasher up and down in the tall crock. My arms would just ache something awful. But oh, the butter was sweet, especially when we spread it on Mama's biscuits. She baked them in a big cast iron wood stove. You don't see those much anymore, mostly just those electric ones with the smooth glass tops. I don't care for those smooth tops, you can't even tell if they're hot just by looking at them. My friend Betty burned her hand real bad on one of those last winter. She had to put aloe vera on it for a month, kept a plant right on her kitchen windowsill... but I suppose if someone asked me to stop with the spoon, I might could. I just haven't really had the occasion to try.\n\nDoctor: When you fold those napkins at church, do you always fold them in exactly the same way each time—the same number of folds, the same direction—or does the way you fold them change from one time to the next?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... exactly the same way? I suppose I don't really pay much mind to how many folds there are. Sometimes it's a few, sometimes more, it just depends on the napkin, I guess. The ones at church are paper, you know, those thin white ones with the little scalloped edges. They always buy them in bulk from the warehouse store out on Highway 9. Pastor Jenkins goes out there once a month in his old station wagon. It's a wood-paneled one, you don't see those much anymore either. The wood on the side is starting to peel off a bit near the back tire. He says he's going to fix it with some epoxy, but he's been saying that since last Christmas. Christmas was lovely, though, we had a big potluck after the service. I brought my sweet potato casserole with the marshmallows on top. The trick is to brown the marshmallows just right, not too dark. If you leave them in the oven even a minute too long, they catch fire. My aunt Lucille set her whole oven on fire back in '82 doing just that. Had to call the fire department and everything... but no, I don't think I fold them the exact same way every time. Just however they end up, really.\n\nDoctor: Has this folding of napkins or holding the spoon caused any real problems for you—like missing getting food at the potluck, or people getting frustrated with you, or you feeling like it's interfering with your life in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, problems... I suppose it has caused a bit of trouble, yes. I've actually had to step back from singing in the choir and doing my church volunteering lately. I just kept mixing up the plans, you see, and people were getting a bit uncomfortable, or they'd have to go behind me and redo the things I was supposed to handle. It's a shame about the choir, really. We wear these lovely royal blue robes. Such a beautiful color. My sister Martha had a dress exactly that color for her youngest boy's wedding. It was an outdoor wedding, out at that farm past the county line. Rained the entire afternoon, just poured buckets. They had a big white tent set up, but it leaked right over the dessert table. Ruined the wedding cake completely. It was a lemon cake with raspberry filling. I always prefer a good pound cake myself, maybe with some fresh strawberries on top. Strawberries were on sale at the grocer this morning, actually. Two pints for three dollars... I should have bought some, but the checkout line was just awfully long.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins, do you ever have any thoughts pop into your head that you don't want to have—like thoughts about germs, or things being 'just right,' or anything like that? Or is it purely just your hands moving without any thoughts involved at all?\n\nPatient: Thoughts about germs? Oh, I don't think so. No, my mind is usually just quiet, or I'm listening to the choir practice or the sermon. It's mostly just my hands doing it on their own, really. Though speaking of germs, they did put up all those hand sanitizer pumps at the church recently. Right by the double doors in the vestibule. They smell awful strong, like cheap rubbing alcohol, if you ask me. \n\nMy husband used to keep a big bottle of rubbing alcohol under the bathroom sink for when he'd nick himself shaving. He used one of those old safety razors, the heavy metal ones where you have to unscrew the top to put the little double-sided blade in. You don't see men using those much anymore, it's all those plastic disposable things now. They just throw them right in the trash after a week. Seems like a terrible waste of plastic. They say all that plastic ends up in the ocean, you know. I saw a program about it on the television, with the sea turtles getting all tangled up in it. I love turtles. We used to catch little box turtles down by the creek when I was a girl, just pick them right up by the shell. You had to watch out for the snapping turtles, though, they'd take your finger right off if you weren't careful... \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the folding and the thoughts. No, I don't have any particular thoughts about it having to be perfect or clean or anything like that. Just my hands moving while I'm sitting there, that's all.\n\nDoctor: When you're at church during the week—like on a Tuesday or Wednesday when there's no potluck or service—do your hands still do this folding and moving on their own, or does it mainly happen just when you're at the potluck table?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... on a Tuesday or Wednesday? I suppose I haven't really noticed it happening during the week. Mostly just when I'm sitting there at the potluck table, waiting for things to get started. They always have the potlucks down in the fellowship hall. It's a nice big room, but the lighting in there is terrible. Those long fluorescent tubes that hum all the time. My husband tried to fix one once, back when we lived in the old house on Elm Street. We had one in the kitchen, right over the sink. He got up on his stepladder, the wooden one with the paint splatters all over it, and he dropped the bulb right on the linoleum. It shattered into a million tiny pieces. Took me forever to sweep it all up, and I still found a piece with my bare foot two days later. You have to be so careful with glass. That's why I always tell my granddaughter to wear her shoes when she goes out to the driveway. She likes to draw with sidewalk chalk out there. She drew a big pink dinosaur last week. It was quite good for a five-year-old, really... but no, I don't think my hands do the folding when I'm just there during the week. Just mostly at the potluck.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins at the potluck, does it feel like your hands are moving completely on their own—like they have a mind of their own—or does it feel more like your hands are just... busy, the way they've always been busy since you were a girl?\n\nPatient: A mind of their own? Well, I don't rightly know if I'd say all that. I suppose they're just... busy. Like you said, I've always had busy hands. My mother used to say idle hands are the devil's workshop, you know. She had me shelling peas on the front porch from the time I was barely big enough to sit in the rocking chair. We grew the most wonderful sugar snap peas. You could just eat them right out of the basket, sweet as candy. \n\nWe had a huge garden out back... grew tomatoes as big as two fists put together. My brother Tommy used to take the salt shaker right out to the garden and eat them like apples. He got a terrible switching for that once, not for eating the tomatoes, but because he dropped my mother's good salt shaker in the dirt and chipped it. It was milk glass, shaped like a little owl. She loved owls, had a whole collection of them in the sitting room. Dusting them was my job on Saturday mornings, and you had to be so careful with the little glass ears, using a special soft cloth so you didn't knock them over. Tommy never had to dust, of course, boys never had to do the inside chores back then. He'd just be out in the yard playing with that old tire swing... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About my hands feeling a certain way? I couldn't really tell you. I just haven't paid much attention to exactly how it feels, they just sort of do it.\n\nDoctor: When your hands are folding those napkins, if you really wanted to stop and sit completely still with your hands in your lap instead, do you think you could do that right away, or would your hands just start up again even though you were trying to keep them still?\n\nPatient: Stop and sit completely still? Oh, I don't rightly know. I don't think I've ever really tried to just... sit there with them in my lap like that. Sitting completely still is awfully hard to do anyway, especially down in the fellowship hall. The folding chairs they have down there are so stiff, not at all like the pews upstairs. \n\nThey finally got cushions for the pews upstairs, you know. Burgundy velvet. They raised money for three whole years at the bake sales to get those cushions. I made my famous pecan pies for every single one of those sales. I use my grandmother's recipe—the secret is just a little dash of dark molasses in with the corn syrup. Makes it rich. My husband couldn't eat it, though, on account of his teeth. He had a terrible time with his dentures in his later years. He got them fitted by a fellow over in Springfield, but they never did sit right on his bottom gums. He used to keep them in a little blue plastic cup on the nightstand. I knocked it over once while I was dusting the lampshade, water went everywhere... ruined a perfectly good doily I'd crocheted myself. Took me three weeks to make that doily. \n\nBut, oh, what was it you asked? About keeping my hands still? I really couldn't say what would happen. I just haven't ever paid it enough mind to try and stop them.\n\nDoctor: If you were sitting at that potluck table and you made a firm decision beforehand—like you told yourself 'today I'm going to keep my hands completely still in my lap the whole time'—do you think you'd be able to stick to that decision, or would your hands just start folding the napkins anyway even though you were trying hard not to?\n\nPatient: Make a firm decision to keep them still? Well... honestly, doctor, that's really why I came to see you in the first place. I just keep doing these odd things at church, and I don't know if I even can stop even if I told myself to. Like, I'll be standing right there by the potluck table, just holding the serving spoon straight up in the air and not moving a muscle, or I'll be folding that exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks are standing right there in line waiting on me. \n\nThey get so fidgety waiting, too, especially when it's macaroni salad day. Mildred always brings her macaroni salad in this giant yellow Tupperware bowl with the burping lid. She puts celery in it, which I've always said is a mistake. Celery gets so stringy if you don't chop it fine enough. My mother never used celery, she used sweet pickles. We'd can them ourselves every August. The kitchen would get so hot, just steaming with vinegar and sugar... you'd be sweating clear through your apron by noon. We had this huge heavy kettle for boiling the mason jars, and you had to use these special metal tongs to lift them out so you didn't scald your fingers. My sister Betty burned her wrist awful bad one year doing that. She had to put butter on it, which they say now you aren't supposed to do, but back then everybody put butter on a burn... \n\nBut, oh, what were you asking? About making a decision to sit still? I just... I don't know. I know people are waiting on me while I'm folding and folding, or just holding that spoon up staring at nothing, but I just don't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over, or holding that spoon up in the air—do you ever feel like 'this doesn't seem right, I wish I wasn't doing this' or does it just feel perfectly natural and okay to you?\n\nPatient: Perfectly natural? Well, I couldn't rightly say if it feels natural or not. I don't really stop to think to myself about wishing I wasn't doing it, it's just... what I'm doing at the time. It's just a spoon, you know. \n\nThough those heavy ones, the serving spoons down at the church, they're real silver plate. Donated by the Henderson family back in nineteen and sixty-two. We used to have a polishing party every spring right before Easter Sunday. All the ladies would sit around in the fellowship hall with old soft rags and that pink polish... Wright's Silver Cream, I think it was. It gets all under your fingernails and turns black as soot when you rub the tarnish off. You have to scrub real hard on the handles because they have this little rose pattern stamped into them, and the tarnish just loves to hide in those little grooves. \n\nMy mother had a silver gravy boat with a rose pattern just like it. We only ever brought it out for Thanksgiving and Christmas. I was the one who had to polish it, of course, because my hands were small enough to get around the little spout. If you didn't get every speck of tarnish off that spout, my mother would make you start all over again from the very beginning. She was particular like that. She used to make my dresses, too, with these tiny little stitches you could barely even see... \n\nBut, oh my, I'm wandering again. What did you ask me? About wishing I wasn't holding the spoon? I just... I don't really have a thought about it one way or the other while it's happening. I just don't notice.\n\nDoctor: When people at church are waiting in line and watching you fold those napkins or hold the spoon, does it bother you that they're watching you do this, or does it feel perfectly fine to you that they can see you doing it?\n\nPatient: Bother me? Well, I don't know if it bothers me exactly. I suppose I don't really pay much mind to them watching. People are always watching at potlucks anyway, seeing who takes the biggest slice of ham or if somebody skips the green beans. \n\nOh, those green beans... Mary Lou always brings them, but she uses the canned ones instead of fresh, and she cooks them until they're just absolute mush. I've told her a hundred times, you have to snap the fresh ones and just steam them for a few minutes so they keep their bite. My garden used to be full of Kentucky Wonders. They grow so tall you have to put up chicken wire for them to climb. One year, a groundhog got under the fence and ate near about half my crop before July. My husband sat out there on the back porch with his BB gun for three nights straight trying to scare that thing off. He never did get him, though. The mosquitoes were so bad that summer, he came in covered in welts. We had to rub calamine lotion all over his arms and neck. It dries all pink and chalky, you know, gets all over the bedsheets. I had to wash those sheets twice with bleach just to get the pink stains out. \n\nBut... what was it you were saying? About folks watching me? I just... I don't really think about it when it's happening. It's just what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that you never had to fold those napkins or hold that spoon again at church, would you feel relieved about that, or would you actually miss doing it?\n\nPatient: Relieved? Or miss it? Well... I couldn't rightly say if I'd feel one way or the other. I've never really thought about it like that. \n\nSpeaking of missing things, though, the only thing I truly miss down at that church is when Reverend Miller used to give the Sunday sermons. Oh, he had a voice like a foghorn, that man. You could hear him clear out in the dirt parking lot if the windows were propped open in the summertime. We didn't have air conditioning back then, just those stiff paper fans they'd hand out with the funeral home's name printed on the back. Benson and Sons, it said, right under a picture of a little white dove. Old Mr. Benson was a quiet man, always wore a heavy gray suit, even in the dead of July. He had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in a farming accident when he was just a boy, working a thresher out in the wheat fields. Those old machines were so dangerous, didn't have any safety guards or nothing. My uncle lost two fingers to a wood saw back in fifty-four, and he had to learn how to hold his coffee cup all over again... \n\nBut, oh, my mind is just running away today. What was it you were asking me about? Would I miss the napkins? I just... I really don't know. I don't think I'd feel much of anything about it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, and people are waiting on you—do you feel embarrassed or ashamed about what you're doing, or does it just feel like a normal part of who you are?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? Ashamed? I don't... I don't rightly know if I'd use those words. I just don't pay it much mind. It's just what I'm doing when I'm standing there. \n\nThough standing in line does make my knees ache something awful these days. Dr. Hodges told me I needed to take that glucosamine stuff, but the pills are the size of horse capsules. I tried cutting them in half with a butter knife once, and the knife slipped right off the counter and chipped the linoleum. We put that linoleum down in nineteen and seventy-eight. It's got a little yellow daisy pattern on it. My husband picked it out because he said it reminded him of his grandmother's kitchen in Tupelo. She used to make the best peach cobbler, with a lattice crust on top that she'd brush with heavy cream and sprinkle with coarse sugar. You have to use the coarse sugar, you see, or it just melts into the dough and you don't get that nice crunch when you bite into it. I tried making it for the church bake sale one year, but I left it in the oven too long because I was busy watching a cardinal build a nest in the dogwood tree outside the kitchen window. They use little bits of dog hair, you know, if they can find it. \n\nBut... oh dear, I've lost the thread again. What did you ask me? About being ashamed? I just... I don't really feel one way or the other about it. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: Before you start folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you ever get a thought or feeling in your mind—like something that bothers you or makes you feel like you need to do it—or does your body just start doing it without any thought coming first at all?\n\nPatient: A thought or a feeling? Well, I don't know if I'd call it a thought. My hands just sort of find their way to the spoon, or the napkins, and then I'm doing it. It's not like a voice telling me to do it, it just happens. \n\nJust like how my hands used to know exactly how to fold the laundry without me even looking. I could fold a fitted sheet in my sleep back when the kids were small. My youngest, Tommy, he always had to have his blankets folded in a perfect square at the foot of his bed, or he'd throw an absolute fit. He was a fussy baby, too with the colic. We tried everything—gripe water, rubbing his belly, even driving him around the block in the old Ford pickup. That truck had a rusted-out muffler, sounded like an airplane taking off, but the rumble of it would put him right to sleep. My husband bought that truck for three hundred dollars from a man down the road who was moving to Ohio to work in a tire factory. They made good money up there, he said, but I couldn't imagine leaving the South for all that snow. They say the snow gets so high up there you have to dig your way out of your own front door. Imagine that, needing a shovel just to go get the morning paper. \n\nOh, listen to me rambling on about snow and trucks. What was it you were asking? About a feeling before I fold the napkins? I don't think so. I just... I don't really notice anything before it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

She kept all the good tablecloths in there, the ones we only used for Thanksgiving and Easter. Easter was always so beautiful at our old church. The pharmacist, young man with glasses, he was very polite. Bag was heavy, though... but no, I don't have any thoughts before I fold them.
